8. Measured and Calculated B-10(n,α) Reaction Rates
 The B-10(n,α) reaction rate was measured as part of the ZPPR-15B shield experiment.
The measurements were performed by placing a flowing gas proportional counter into a drawer
at six locations in the shield, radial reflector, blanket, and core of the ZPPR-15B BCNA shielding
experiments (see Section 7) for a summary description of the BCNA configuration. The counter
was a cylindrical back-to-back (BTB) counter divided into two chambers. One chamber
contained a rectangular deposit of enriched boron (96.5% B-10) on a stainless steel backing. The
boron deposit was sufficiently thin such that the boron could be regarded as infinitely dilute for
analysis purposes. The other chamber was empty. Counts were recorded in both chambers of the

 43

BTB counter. Counts in the empty chamber were subtracted from the total number of counts in
the boron chamber to correct for gamma-induced counts in the boron chamber. There was only
one B-10 BTB proportional counter. Thus, the B-10(n,α) reaction rate was measured in the
ZPPR-15B shield experiment by moving this counter from one matrix location to another in six
successive loadings (L127 to L132). Figure 20 shows the core loading and counter positions in
the ZPPR matrix for these measurements. Table 35 and Table 36 show the correspondence
between the loading number and counter position. The B-10 proportional counter was located in
half 1 and the matrix loading in half 2 was not changed during these measurements. Similar to
the neutron spectrum measurement, the DIF3D and MCNP bias for each loading was consistent
with that observed with the critical reference loading of ZPPR-15B.

Figure 20. Matrix Locations (Column Location is Highlighted) for the B-10(n,α) Reaction Rate

 Table 35 shows the comparison of the measured values with the DIF3D-VARIANT
calculations, and Table 36 shows the corresponding comparison for the MCNP calculations. The
exact boron mass in the BTB counter deposit and the efficiency of the counter are unknown, so
it was not possible to determine absolute reaction rates

 44

 The experimenters normalized the reported values to 10.0 in matrix location 158-35, and
the calculated results were normalized in the same way. In the MCNP calculation, the BTB
counter volume was explicitly modeled and results were obtained with approximately one billion
histories via the FM4 tally multiplier with reaction number 107 (i.e., n,α reaction) for the B-10
isotope. For the DIF3D reaction rate, the group-wise fluxes obtained from the VARIANT core
calculation in 230 energy groups was combined with B-10 detector cross sections. Detector cross
sections were obtained in the outer core region from MC2 by introducing a very thin slab at the
counter location in one of the lattice cell calculations. In the shield and simulated pool regions,
the B-10 counter was simply homogenized with the existing composition at that point (infinite
dilution) noting that 0D calculations are done in MC 2 for these regions. Only location 158-27 in
Table 35 has a low enough error to consider DIF3D to be reliably accurate where the remaining
points show a consistent increasing negative bias as the measurement device was moved outward
into the radial reflector, the B4C/Na shield, and the simulated pool region. Inspection of both the
MCNP and DIF3D tend to indicate that both are rather poor in the outer regions. It is important
to note that the statistical error in the MCNP calculation does increase significantly in the ex-
core regions making the value calculated in the sodium pool unreliable.

 To explore this further, the neutron spectrum results were analyzed. Figure 21 shows the
neutron spectrum calculated with DIF3D at each location where the B-10(n,α) rates were
measured. The spectrum from the core position, loading 132, has very low levels of low energy
neutrons present. Loadings 128 to 130 all reside in the heavy borated region surrounding the core
and one can see significantly depressed low energy neutrons due to the absorption in boron.

 45

Similarly, the simulated sodium pool region, loading 127, has a depressed thermal tail although
not as negligible as the other regions. In the modified radial reflector region, loading 131, the
spectrum has the largest thermal neutron tail of all positions. Figure 22 displays the B-10(n,α)
detector cross section obtained with MC2 and, except for the core region, the B-10(n,α) cross
section is effectively identical. Figure 23 shows the contribution from each energy group to the
B-10(n,α) reaction rate at each location. From this figure, one can see that the largest
contributions are between 200eV – 200keV for all loadings but important contributions are
present below 200eV (especially for loadings 127 and 131). Given the B-10 reaction is not
substantially perturbed when placed in each region, it is likely that the flux solution produced by
DIF3D is causing the error which means the other isotopic cross sections, or even the
deterministic modeling approach itself, are to blame for the underlying errors. Given the MCNP
results are not a perfect match to the experiment, it is likely a mixture of both.

9. Analysis of Foil Measurements
 Several types of foils were irradiated in loadings 123 and 134 of ZPPR-15B and loading
203 of ZPPR-15D to measure spatial reaction rate distributions.

9.1 Foil Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loadings 123 and 134
 The reaction rate distribution measurements were performed in two shield
configurations. In the SSNA configuration, Foil irradiation measurements were performed in
loading 123 of ZPPR-15B. In the BCNA configuration, foil measurements were performed in
loading 134 of ZPPR-15B. See Section 7) for a summary description of the SSNA and BCNA
configuration. The keff results for each loading with foils was found to be consistent with the
critical reference results reported earlier.
 In both loading 123 and 134, small metal foils were irradiated to measure reaction rate
distributions along the X-axis in the core, blanket, reflector and shield zones. Four types of foils
were used in the ZPPR-15B shield experiments. U-235(n,f) reactions were measured with U-235
foils. The U-235 foils were 12.7 mm in diameter and 0.112 mm thick. U-238(n,f) and U-238(n,γ)
reactions were measured with U-238 foils. The diameter and thickness of the U-238 foils were
12.7 mm and 0.140 mm, respectively. U-235 and U-238 foils were coated with Kel-F and were
covered on the top and bottom with 0.13 mm aluminum disks. Pu-239 foils were used to measure
Pu-239(n,f) reactions (no Pu-239 foil measurements were performed in L134). The diameter and
thickness of the Pu-239 foils were 10.8 mm and 0.127 mm, respectively, and the nominal
plutonium mass was ~150 mg. The Pu-239 foils were clad in 0.025 mm aluminum. In-115(n,γ)
and (n,n’) reactions were measured with indium foils. The diameter and thickness of the indium
foils were 12.7 mm and 0.25 mm, respectively.
 The measurements were performed by placing 1 – 3 foils into holes in thin stainless steel
or aluminum holders which were inserted between plates in drawers at selected locations in half
1 of ZPPR-15B. The foils were then irradiated, removed from the reactor and counted to
determine reaction rates. The foils in the holders were centered at the mid-height of the drawer.
The axial positions of the foils from the interface between half 1 and half 2 were 63.1 mm for
the U-235 foils, 77.0 mm for the U-238 foils and 90.8 mm for the Pu-239 foils. When indium
foils were irradiated, they replaced Pu-239 foils at the 90.8 mm position.
 In Figure 24 and Figure 25, the dashed line indicates the line along which the foils were
placed in half 1 of ZPPR-15B loading 123, the SSNA configuration, and in half 1 of ZPPR-15B
loading 134, the BCNA configuration, respectively. The measured foil data was reported in two
forms in the ZPPR reports: mapping foil and cell averaged results [25]. In the present analysis,
only the mapping foil data is computed as the cell averaged data is considered less precise.
Details on the location of the mapping foils in the ZPPR-15 experiments and in the DIF3D
geometric model are given in Appendix D. Appendix D1 provides the measured reaction rates
and the positions of the mapping foils in loading 123 of ZPPR-15B while Appendix D2 provides
the measured reaction rates and positions of the mapping foils in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B.

 48

 Figure 24. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 123 of ZPPR-15B
 For the DIF3D calculation of reaction rates, the MC2 slab lattice geometries generated
by BuildZPRmodel were modified to include narrow foil regions at the specific locations of the
measurement in each drawer type. For blanket, reflector and shield zones, the foil material was
added at infinite dilution to the existing 0D MC2 input to generate cross section data. In the
blanket region, the U-238 present in the drawer is indistinguishable from the foil and thus the
foil cross sections effectively become the drawer U-238 cross section. To get the calculated
reaction rates, the DIF3D flux solution is evaluated at the centroid of the exact foil location and
then combined with the foil cross sections.
 Two normalizations were adopted for the calculated and measured results. In the first
approach, each set of calculated reaction rate data is normalized by calculating a normalizing
factor with the calculated and measured value that is closest to the core center (normalization
A). This normalization factor is different for each reaction type and is used, respectively, to

 49

normalize all of the other calculated results for that reaction type. In the second approach, a
single normalization factor is obtained from the computed and measured U-235 fission reaction
rate measurement nearest the core center (normalization B). In normalization B, this single
normalization factor is used to scale all of the reaction rate measurements in that loading.

 Figure 25. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
 With normalization A, the different reaction rates (U-235(n,f), U-238(n,f), U-238 (n,γ),
Pu-239(n,f), In-115(n,γ), and In-115(n,n’)) are not referenced to a single flux level and thus are
relative reaction rate comparisons for a given reaction rate type. With normalization B, all of the
reaction rates are all consistently normalized with the way the measurement was intended.
Normalization B should expose more problems with the cross section data and foil cross section
generation methodology than Normalization A.

 50

 Table 37 through Table 41 show the calculated results for the reaction rate distributions
in loading 123 of ZPPR-15B. Results for both normalization schemes are provided along with
the measurement errors. Figure 26 through Figure 30 graph each reaction rate distribution result
using normalization A for loading 123 of ZPPR-15B. In each figure, the reaction rate values are
indexed to the left axis while the error distribution (“σ relative (Exp.)” is the measurement error)
is indexed to the right axis. The figures also indicate the reactor zones where the measurements
are performed: inner core (IC), outer core (OC), blanket (BK), reflector (RF), simulated pool
(SP), SSNA shield.
 Starting with the U-235 fission rate distribution in Table 37 and Figure 26, one can see
that the foil distribution has a very low error in the core and blanket regions. However, in the
radial reflector and shielding regions, the error rapidly grows to greater than 20%. No definitive
reason is available to explain this error although it is suspected that the reflector cross sections
are leading to a miss prediction of the flux levels and thus the erroneous reaction rate prediction
as also observed in past studies [27][27].

 Continuing with the U-238 reaction rate distributions in Table 38, Table 39, Figure 27
and Figure 28, one finds slightly higher errors in the fission rate of U-238, compared with the U-
235 results. For the capture reaction rate of U-238, the error increases considerably in the blanket
to more than 5%, but overall these foil reaction rate results are still considered good. The last set
of foils irradiated in this loading are In-115 the results of which are given in Table 40, Table 41,
Figure 29, and Figure 30. As seen, the In-115 foils have a considerably larger measurement
uncertainty and it is therefore no surprise to see that the predictions from DIF3D have enormous
errors. In particular, the In-115 inelastic scattering measurements are effectively unusable for
validation work given the inaccuracy in the measured and DIF3D results. The preceding U-235
and U-238 foils are sufficient to show that the DIF3D reaction rate predictions are rather accurate
in the core and blanket regions with notable poor performance in the outer reflector and shielding
regions.

 51

 Figure 30. In-115(n,γ) Reaction Rate Distribution in Loading 123 of ZPPR-15B

 To better understand the observed discrepancies between the calculated and experimental
U-235(n,f) reaction rates in the reflector, the neutron spectrum and contribution by energy group
was investigated. Figure 31 shows that in the SSNA zone at the interface with the outer core, a
peak in the neutron spectrum is observed at low energies (<1 keV) as expected. This peak

 56

vanishes gradually with respect to outward movement in the SSNA zone. Figure 32 shows that
the U-235 foil cross section increases below 1 keV and that there are significant differences at
the two spatial points. Finally, Figure 33 shows that the largest contributions to the U-235(n,f)
rate in the SSNA shield region come from the 10eV – 1000eV energy range. This tends to
indicate that the errors in the shielding region is due to base errors in the U-235 cross section
data or mistakes in the multi-group cross section processing methodology used for this work. No
results using MCNP can be obtained in these regions and thus further analysis is left for future
work.

 Figure 33. Energy Group Breakdown of the U-235(n,f) Reaction Rate in Loading 123

 Table 42 through Table 47 tabulate the reaction rate results for loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
for both normalization schemes. Figure 34 through Figure 39 graph the reaction rate results using
normalization A in loading 134 of ZPPR-15B. As was the case for loading 123, each figure
indexes the reaction rate on the left axis and the error on the right axis. Each figure also denotes
where each measurement lies with respect to inner core (IC), outer core (OC), blanket (BK),
reflector (RF), simulated pool (SP), and BCNA shield. The location of foil measurements was
effectively identical to that used in loading 123. It is important to note that the alteration in the
shielding material (from SSNA to BCNA) makes the measurements in the two loadings quite
different.

 Starting with the U-235 fission reaction rate distribution, a quick comparison of Figure
34 with Figure 26 indicates that the same problems exist in the shielding regions as seen in
loading 123. In the core and blanket regions the DIF3D results are still accurate while the radial
reflector results are somewhat more accurate in loading 134. Additional follow-on work using
different space-angle approximations did not appreciably change the foil measurement results.

 Continuing with the U-238 foil results in Table 43, Table 44, Figure 35, and Figure 36,
one can see that the fission measurements are accurate in the outer core and blanket regions but
considerable errors are found in the other regions. One interesting aspect is that the stainless
steel/sodium pool mixture adjacent to the left side of the experiment has considerably less error
than the solid stainless-steel reflector located on the right side. This would seem to indicate that
there is a fundamental issue with the multi-group cross section data for stainless steel but no
further investigation was considered as part of this work. The U-238 capture reaction rate results
are very similar to those observed in loading 123 with less than 10% error in the core and blanket.

 Figure 36. U-238(n,γ) Reaction Rate Distribution in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
 No Pu-239 foil results were included in loading 123 and thus their addition to loading
134 should be considered beneficial. From Figure 37 one finds that the Pu-239 reaction rate
prediction is quite accurate (less than 1.2% of error) but the foils were only placed in the core.
Thus they only provide supplemental information with regard to the information provided by the
U-235 foil measurement and indicate that the foil cross section generation process in the core
works for another actinide.
 The In-115 reaction rate distributions are included here but are discarded for the same
lack of sufficient measurement accuracy as was done in loading 123. Overall, the calculated
reaction rate distributions in loading 134 are good for the core and blanket regions and rather
poor for the remaining regions. Combined with 123 and the neutron spectrum measurements

 61

from earlier in this manuscript, these results indicate that DIF3D is doing well at predicting the
flux distribution within the core and blanket regions. The reflector and shield regions are not as
good noting that propagation of the errors in the reflector into outer regions could be partly to
blame for the errors in the outer regions.

 As was done for loading 123, the neutron spectrum and group wise contribution for the
U-235(n,f) reaction rate was studied. Figure 40 and Figure 41 display the neutron spectra in
several parts of the geometry. As can be seen, the SSNA results have a peak in the neutron
spectrum at low energies (<1 keV) as expected. This peak vanishes gradually as the measurement
point moves outward into the BCNA zone.

A thermal peak is observed again in the simulated pool zone. Figure 42 shows that the U-235
fission cross section increases below 1 keV. Finally, Figure 43 and Figure 44 show that the
largest contributions to the U-235 fission rate is from the 10eV – 1000eV energy range. As was
the case for loading 123, this indicates that the errors in the shielding region are due to base
errors in the U-235 cross section data or mistakes in the multi-group cross section processing
methodology used for this work. No results using MCNP can be obtained in these regions and
thus further analysis is left for future work.

9.2 Foil Measurements in ZPPR-15D Loadings 203
 Foil measurements were also done for loading 203 of ZPPR-15D. Unlike loading 123
and 134, loading 203 included reaction rate distributions along the X, Y, and Z axes in the core,
radial blanket and radial reflector. The axial (Z) reaction rate distributions were measured at
three locations labeled Z1, Z2, and Z3. The same U-235(n,f), U-238(n,f) and U-238(n,γ), and
Pu-239 (n,f) foils were used in these measurements using the same loading methodology. Figure
45 shows the general placement of foils in the X and Y directions and the three drawers chosen
for the Z direction measurements. All foils were placed in half 1.

 67

 Figure 45. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 203 of ZPPR-15D

 Similar to loading 123 and 134, the measured foil data were reported in basic and cell
average values. In the present analysis, only the basic data are analyzed for which the measured
reaction rates and positions of the foils are provided in Appendix D3. The same two
normalization strategies used for loading 123 and 134 were used here except normalization B is
based upon the Pu-239 fission rate instead of U-235. In the case of normalization A, the
calculated rates along the three axial directions are all normalized by setting the value at the
closest location to the core center (i.e., first measured value of Z1 direction) identical to the
corresponding measured one. Table 48 through Table 67 tabulate the reaction rate distribution
results for all foils used in loading 203 where the error from using both normalizations schemes
is displayed. Because Pu-239 foils were irradiated, the common spectral indices can be computed
at some of the foil positions which are tabulated in Table 68 through Table 82. The spectral
indices included are the U-238(n,f)/Pu-239(n,f), U-238(n,γ)/ Pu-239(n,f) and U-235(n,f)/Pu-

 68

239(n,f) reaction rate ratios where it is assumed that the foils underwent the same irradiation
conditions.
 Figure 46 through Figure 65 display the reaction rate distribution results using
normalization scheme A from the tabulated results. The reaction rate values are indexed to the
left axis while the error is indexed to the right axis. Each figure also indicates the reactor region
where the foil measurement was located: inner core (IC), outer core (OC), blanket (BK), reflector
(RF). There was no special shielding region in ZPPR-15D.
 From Figure 46 through Figure 50, the calculated Pu-239(n,f) foils have errors that are
within or slightly above the experimental uncertainties. In past studies [28] the uncertainty on
the calculated value of a normalized reaction rate in the core of a typical sodium fast reactor was
estimated to be on the order of 1%, without accounting for the uncertainty from the foil cross
sections. The trend of the results indicates a slight increase in error in the blanket zone (DM 501)
which is partly attributable to larger measurement errors and a different foil cross section
methodology. For the same reason, the Pu-239 reaction rate distribution along the Z direction at
Z3 (outer core boundary) show slightly larger discrepancies compared with the reaction rate
distribution at Z1 (inner core). Finally, the measured Pu-239 reaction rate distribution along the
X axis, Figure 46, indicate two small drops near drawer 902 and 903 which is not reproduced by
DIF3D. Given no meaningful investigation to understand this can be done, the calculated result
would seem more believable given the actual content of drawer 902 and 903 are effectively
identical to the surrounding drawers.
 Continuing with the U-238(n,f) reaction rate distribution, the results are plotted in Figure
51 through Figure 55. As was the case with the U-238(n,f) reaction rate distributions in loading
123 and 134, the DIF3D results agree well with the measured values in the core with a maximum
error <10% and most actual errors <5%. However, the error increases significantly in the blanket
region having a maximum near 70% error at the outermost measurement in Z3 and generally
greater than 5% error overall. The Z axis traverses in Figure 53 through Figure 55 show larger
errors than the X and Y axis measurements, but the measurement error also increases
significantly reducing the confidence in the blanket measurements. Similar to the Pu-239
measurements, there are notable peaks in the X and Y axis measurements that correspond to
drawers 916, 923, 925, 926 and 928. The measurement error is noted to also increase although
the overall impact is minor. In this case the DIF3D results mimic these peaks and close inspection
shows that these drawers consist of two column fuel plates instead of a single column fuel plate
and thus the peaks make sense.
 Figure 56 through Figure 60 display the U-238(n,γ) reaction rate distribution results.
Much like the U-238(n,f) results, the U-238(n,γ) results indicate a very accurate solution within
the core and reduced accuracy in the blanket. However, the maximum error in these results
reaches <10% in the blanket regions and the experimental error is also significantly less. The Z
axis traverses in Figure 58 through Figure 60 also show considerable differences from the
preceding U-238(n,f) results. While the DIF3D results still have significant errors in the blanket
region, overall, they are rather acceptable.
 Figure 61 and Figure 62 display the X and Y axis U-235(n,f) reaction rate distribution
results. As was the case for the U-235(n,f) in loading 123 and 134 of ZPPR-15B, the U-235(n,f)
in ZPPR-15D results are very accurate in the core and blanket regions and the errors increase

 69

considerably in the radial reflector region. As was concluded for the previous loadings, the U-
235(n,f) foil cross sections are expected to be reasonably accurate and the flux magnitude and
spectrum are believed to be incorrect because of problems with the stainless steel cross sections.
Figure 63 through Figure 65 display the Z axis traverse results at the three locations in Figure
45. As can be seen, the results are consistent with the X and Y axis results with excellent match
in the core region and slightly worse results in the blanket and reflector regions. Overall, these
results indicate acceptable results for DIF3D.
 The spectral indices results displayed in Table 68 through Table 82 are part of the
reported measurement data. These results are based upon normalization B and it should be no
surprise that the errors of each case are larger than the displayed reaction rate data. Because most
of the available data is in the core region, the errors range from 3% to 7% in most cases. As the
data points approach the reflector zones, the error degrades consistent with the regular reaction
rate data.

 As was done in loading 123 and 134, analysis of the neutron spectrum and the energy
group contributions to the U-235(n,f) reaction rate were carried out at a few measurement

 93

locations. Figure 66 shows that in the radial reflector zone at the interface with the blanket, a
peak in the neutron spectrum is observed at low energies (<1 keV) consistent with the previous
results. This peak reduces gradually with outward movement towards the reflector zone. Figure
67 shows that the U-235 foil fission cross section increases below 1 keV and is relatively
consistent between the inner and outer core. Finally, Figure 68 shows that the largest
contributions to the U235(n,f) reaction rate in the radial reflector are located in the 10eV – 1 keV
energy range. This result is also consistent with the previous results and indicates that the most
likely cause for the errors is the flux distribution more than the multi-group U-235 cross section.
No effort was made to reproduce any of these results using MCNP given the computational effort
required to complete the task.

 Except for the In-115 foils from ZPPR-15B, all of the preceding foil measurement
comparisons indicate that the DIF3D calculations are accurate within the core region for cross
sections that are well known. Combined with the spectrum measurements, this work can be used
to validate the accuracy of the flux spectrum and neutron power distribution obtained with
DIF3D.
10.Analysis of Pu-242 and Np-237 Fission Rate Measurements
 Back-to-back flowing-gas ionization chambers were used to measure the threshold
fission rates of Np-237 and Pu-242 in loading 141 of ZPPR-15B. The chambers were those
described elsewhere [25]. Two chambers, each with one Pu-242 deposit and one Np-237 deposit,
were irradiated simultaneously, one in the sodium pool outside the shield and one in the radial
blanket in the side of the assembly away from the shield. The chambers were accommodated in
drawers by removing most of the materials from the first 152 mm of the drawers. A 12.7 mm
wide and a 12.7 mm high channel was also provided for cables and gas tubes. Fission rates in
the Pu-242 and Np-237 were determined by correcting the total chamber counts for counts
contributed by small amounts of fissionable impurities in the deposits. The deposit compositions
were determined in mass spectrometric and alpha counting measurements. Fission rates for the
impurity isotopes were provided by calculations. The deposits are sufficiently thin that infinitely
dilute cross sections are appropriate for calculations comparisons with measurements.

 For the Np-237 and Pu-242 rates, uncertainties were estimated based upon counting
statistics and the ability to separate counts due to fission events from counts due to alpha
emission. Uncertainties in the impurity corrections to the Pu-242 rates were assumed to be 10%
of the correction, which was small compared to other uncertainty sources. The estimated
uncertainties for neptunium were 2% in the blanket and 3.6% outside the shield. For plutonium,
they were 1.5% in the blanket and 5% outside the shield. The Np-237 and Pu-242 threshold
fission reaction rates were measured in the sodium pool (SP) outside of the shield (location 158-
19) and in the radial blanket (RB) on the side of the assembly away from the shield (location

 95

158-67), as shown in Figure 139 of Appendix A. The k eff result for this loading is consistent with
the critical reference loading for ZPPR-15B. The measured rates and calculated values with
DIF3D are given in Table 83 along with the location of the chambers in the ZPPR-15B loading
and deterministic model (see Appendix D for details).

 The measured Pu-242 values in Table 83 have been corrected for contributions to the
count rate from Pu-239, Pu-240, and Pu-241 which were present in the deposit in small
concentrations. The largest correction, about 4%, was from Pu-241 in the simulated sodium pool
location all of which were based upon calculated reaction rates. The total corrections were ~5%
in the sodium pool and ~2% in the blanket. No corrections were needed for the Np-237
measurements. Because there is insufficient information to compute an absolute reaction rate,
the ratio of the two measurements is used to compare the results and is included in Table 83. As
can be seen, the measurement indicates a similar ratio between the Pu-242 and Np-237 results.
The DIF3D result also produces a similar ratio for the two isotopes, although clearly not as
consistent as the measured one, but the magnitude of that ratio is ~60% below the ratio of the
measured values. This outcome and actual type of measurement (shielding) tends to make this
measurement not useful for validation purposes noting that the effort required to complete this
task is trivial compared with the preceding foil reaction rates.
 To better understand the observed discrepancies the neutron spectrum and energy group
contribution to the Pu-242(n,f) and Np-237(n,f) reaction rates was analyzed. Figure 69 displays
the DIF3D neutron spectrum at each measurement location and indicates a significant
thermalization of the neutron spectrum in the simulated pool.

from high neutron energies >20keV. This can be contrasted with the energy group breakdown in
the sodium pool shown in Figure 72 where there is a non-negligible contribution at ~1 keV.
Given the preceding foil analysis similar to this indicated problems with the flux magnitude in
the sodium pool, it is likely that this aspect also dominates the error observed in this calculation.

11. Analysis of Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth Measurements
 Axial expansion experiments were performed in ZPPR-15B and ZPPR-15D. In each
case a segmented drawer was constructed. The back of the segmented drawer was connected to
a spring-loaded cable that could be pulled away from the axial core centerline to introduce gaps
between drawer segments. Releasing the cable eliminated the gaps between drawer segments,
so the segmented drawer was essentially identical to any other core drawer when the cable was
released. The small gaps introduced between drawer segments simulated core axial expansion
as closely as possible given the constraint that the core materials consisted of rigid plates of fixed
sizes. The segmented drawer design allowed experimenters to measure the reactivity worths of
the reference and expanded configurations in one reactor run.

 98

 The ZPPR-15B and ZPPR-15D experiments both had a base configuration with no gaps
and one or multiple perturbed configurations where gaps between sections of each drawer were
opened. Figure 73 shows a pictorial representation of a standard core drawer (upper picture), the
base case of the axial expansion drawer (center), and the perturbed or expanded case of the
expansion drawer (lower). As can be seen, with every drawer there is a core, blanket, and
reflector region, the dimensions of which can be found in the ZPPR-15 experiment details [21]
to [26].

 Figure 73. Visualization of the Axial Expansion Drawer in Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

An existing core drawer was cut into several pieces, small stainless steel plates (0.0762 cm
thickness for ZPPR-15B, 0.0508 cm thickness for ZPPR-15D) were attached to the open ends of
each segment to prevent axial shifting of the plates during the experiment. This causes the axial
expansion drawer to have a slight misalignment at each major region boundary compared with
the standard core drawer geometry.

11.1 Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth of Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

 The segmented fuel drawer for loading 117 of ZPPR-15B is divided into three parts, as
shown in Figure 73. The first segment is the first 6 inches of the core while the second segment
is the next 6 inches of the core (6 – 12 in). The third segment is the remainder of the core region
(12 – 18 in) plus the axial blanket (18 – 31 in) and axial reflector (31 – 36 in). All of the fuel
plates used for the core in the segmented drawer were ~6 inches long. The plates used for the
axial blanket and axial reflector in the segmented drawer were consistent with respect to the
materials loaded in adjacent core drawers.

 For the expansion experiment in loading 117, the size of gap between each segment was
0.25 inches. Modeling the gap explicitly is difficult for DIF3D as it cannot handle “void” regions.
In order to use the transport options of DIF3D, one easy solution is to introduce a small smearing
zone at each axial boundary, as illustrated in Figure 74. In this scheme, four axial regions are
added to the typical three region homogenization approach. The axial size of the four
homogenization zones (A2, A4, A6 and A8) are user defined and the values in Table 84 were
used for this work. As seen, five models were constructed and analyzed. The segment boundary
of the expanded drawer is shifted ~0.5 inches from the original core boundary, and because it is

 99

desirable to keep the boundary consistent with the current homogenization zone the minimum
homogenization zone should be no less than 0.5 inch.

  Figure 74. Auxiliary Homogenization Zones Introduced for Modeling the Axial Expansion

 Table 84. Auxiliary Homogenization Zone Sizing for the Axial Expansion Experiment in
 Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B.
 Axial Length (inches)
 Model 1 Model 2 Model 3 Model 4 Model 5
                  A2     2.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A4     2.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A6     1.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A8       1.0        1.0           0.75            0.6        0.52

 The default input to BuildZPRmodel is the un-expanded configuration of this drawer.
Thus, with proper input, BuildZPRmodel can calculate the atom densities of all of the axial
regions of this drawer (i.e. A1 to A10) directly for use in the DIF3D model of the full core case.
For the expanded configuration, the introduction of a 0.25 inch gap at boundary 1 and 2 and 0.5
inch gap at boundary 3 and 4 can again be applied to the unexpanded configuration setup. Region
specific cross section data was not generated for the expanded model, but the number densities
were adjusted and the isotopic cross section data from the unexpanded model were used.

 To facilitate the calculation of the expanded drawer configuration, dummy ZPPR
problems were created and used in BuildZPRmodel. These dummy problems were only used to
produce the atom density results for each homogenized zone and do not make any physical sense
with regard to a ZPPR assembly. With respect to Figure 74, at boundaries 1 and 2, the dummy
problem introduces a homogenization zone that is 0.25 inch smaller such that the total mass of
the expanded drawer can be preserved properly. At boundaries 3 and 4, the dummy model uses
a homogenization zone that is 0.5 inch smaller. For clarity, the unexpanded and expanded drawer
model both needs to merge some of the material from zone A5 (core) with zone A6 (blanket). In
the dummy model, the boundary is moved to properly obtain the homogenized composition for
the expanded configuration.

 Using the five models described above, Table 85 provides the calculated axial expansion
reactivity worth for loading 117 of ZPPR-15B where the measured result is -0.641 pcm ± 5.87%.

 100

As can be seen, as the size of the homogenization region is reduced, the accuracy of the predicted
worth improves. At the very finest two models (0.6 and 0.52 inches), the error is observed to
increase beyond the experimental uncertainty indicating that one can potentially get a much
worse answer if a fully heterogeneous calculation was attempted. The impact of using different
energy group structures and angular approximations had little impact on the DIF3D results and
other calculations using different spatial approximations (not shown for brevity) yielded a similar
conclusion.

 Based upon the preceding results, model 4 is closest to the measurement even though
model 5 should be more accurate as it is more rigorous with regard to the material movement.
This indicates that there is a limit to the homogenization methodology and there likely is some
cancellation of error occurring with the homogenization itself. After some study, the movement
of fuel materials into the void region was determined to be the primary problem and this is not
accurate with regard to the underlying expansion experiment.

 To remedy the situation, two smaller homogenization zones are introduced at boundaries
1 and 2, termed Model 6 in this report. Given well selected homogenization zone sizing, the
artificial movement of fuel (the high worth part of the structure being moved) material into the
gapped region can be minimized for both the un-expanded and expanded configurations. This of
course introduces smaller mesh sizes and constructs a model which is closer to the real
heterogeneous problem (the components of the steel drawer end plates are still smeared into the

 101

gapped regions). Figure 75 illustrates the final homogenization methodology utilized for the
axial expansion experiment in loading 117 of ZPPR-15B.

 Figure 75. Model 6 Homogenization Scheme for Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

 The axial boundaries used in the DIF3D model are provided in Table 86 where the
smallest mesh size is 0.250 inches. For this model, the transport options of DIF3D converge
when using a 33 group cross section library but do not when using a 116 group cross section
library thus diffusion theory results are provided. The DIF3D axial expansion reactivity worth
results are tabulated in Table 87. As can be seen, the results for all of the calculations are
consistent and ~15% above the measured results. It is not clear if this error is due to the
homogenization or other modeling issues (i.e. cross section data).

 As stated, even when using Model 6, the existing model of the gap still involves smearing
of the structural materials (parts of drawer and some plate cladding) into the gap regions. Thus,
the final model attempted, Model 7, focuses on strict preservation of the material boundaries.
For this explicit model, each segment of the drawer is homogenized to form a single composition
and thus have identical compositions in the expanded and unexpanded case. In the expanded
case, the matrix tube composition is simply inserted using the region specific cross sections
needed to define the content.
 Adding new spatial boundaries between the expanded and unexpanded models is
equivalent to spatial mesh refinement. In general, this would be a minor impact on the overall
solution and would not be a concern for problems such as a control rod insertion. In this particular
case, the worth of the expansion is so small that spatial mesh refinement was observed to
introduce substantial errors into the solution. Thus, both expanded and unexpanded models must
utilize the identical axial meshing even though the unexpanded case does not require those
additional mesh boundaries.

 Table 88 provides the axial boundaries used for the two configurations of the expandable
drawer for better clarity on the explicit modeling. DIF3D is able to solve this problem using
transport when using a 33 group cross section library but all higher energy group libraries diverge
and only diffusion theory solutions are possible.

Table 89. Comparing of the results for Model 7 with Model 6 (the homogenized boundary case)
results in Table 87, one can see that they are very consistent when using transport but quite

 103

inconsistent when using diffusion theory. Thus Model 6 is likely more reliable for diffusion
theory which is known to have difficulties with large material changes.

 The resulting error in the DIF3D results is difficult to resolve. Past experience with
DIF3D would suggest that it should produce an excellent result as the expansion itself constitutes
a minor change in the flux distribution. In this regard, the actual worth is caused by the movement
of the material rather than a 1st or 2nd order change in the flux. As a consequence, the preceding
would indicate that there is a problem with the model construction but as can be seen, multiple
approaches were taken and all tend to indicate that the DIF3D approach is still yielding an
incorrect solution. Thus a comparative computational capability is desired. From experience,
using a MCNP type approach would require computational resources that are way beyond
today’s capabilities. Similarly, experience has shown that the perturbation theory options of
MCNP are generally unreliable for this type of problem. Thus a comparative calculation to assess
whether this is a problem with the cross sections or a problem with the DIF3D model is not
straightforward.

 To overcome this problem, a hypothetical loading, termed Loading 117mod48, is
constructed which is solvable via MCNP. In 117mod48, 48 additional expandable fuel drawers
are introduced in a regular pattern on the same matrix side as the expandable drawer as illustrated
in Figure 76. Thus, in the perturbation calculation, 49 expandable fuel drawers undergo the
expansion leading to and expansion worth >30 pcm of reactivity noting the more centrally
located expansion drawers will generate larger a reactivity worth than the measured -0.6 pcm.
The selection of these drawers was done without consideration for single or double column fuel
drawers and thus the criticality state of the reactor is different from the original Loading 117.

 The calculated worth using the explicit geometry model of Loading 117mod48 with
MCNP was -40±4.24 pcm where the 3 sigma error is provided (10.6%). Using all seven
described models in DIF3D, the results for Loading 117mod48 were calculated and tabulated in
Table 90. The calculated errors are done with respect to MCNP as there is no experimental
measurement result for this problem. Because of the high MCNP error, most of the DIF3D results
are within the error bars. Starting with the single homogenization zone approach used in Model
1 to 5, one can see that the results are relatively accurate for the first two models, but Model 4
and 5 both underpredict the reactivity worth considerably. This is consistent with the Loading
117 results shown earlier in Table 85 and thus the conclusion that the single homogenization
methodology is not reliable is supported by these results.

 105

 Continuing with the Model 6 and 7 results, one can see that the two homogenization
boundary result is twice as accurate (~3.6% error versus 7.1% error) as the model that preserves
the explicit boundaries. The diffusion theory results for both cases are notably worse than the 33
transport based results consistent with that observed in the Loading 117 results. This supports
the conclusion that diffusion theory calculations are not reliable for Model 6 and Model 7 and
one should use transport theory. Given the error between MCNP and DIF3D is less than 10%
and the worth is still relatively small, one can have greater confidence that the errors observed
in the Loading 117 results of Table 87 and Table 89 are likely due to cross section errors or some
unforeseen error in the experiment itself or interpretation of the experiment with regard to
constructing a MCNP and DIF3D model.

 In conclusion, the single homogenization methodology (Model 1 to 5) is not wise and
should not be used for these types of expansion calculations. While the two homogenization zone
model (Model 6) is accurate, it is more difficult to setup and thus simply easier to build Model
7 which preserves the material regions in both configurations. While the calculated error with
respect to the measurement is large, it is not possible at present to identify a root cause for the
issue given the available information.

11.2 Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth for Loading 206 of ZPPR-15D

 In ZPPR-15D, the expandable drawer was segmented into nine pieces and three different
experiments were done with it as illustrated in Figure 77. The nominal segment length was ~2
inches for each of the first eight core segments. The remaining ~2 inches of the core, ~13 inches
of axial blanket and ~5 inches of axial reflector were placed in the ninth segment which had a
total length of ~20 inches. There was a 0.020 in (0.0508 cm) steel plate fixed to the open end of
each segment to prevent axial motion of the plates in each segment. Based upon the results for
Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B, only Model 7 is used with DIF3D when computing the axial
expansion worth of Loading 206 of ZPPR-15D.
 As stated, three axial expansion experiments were performed with two of them
introducing 2 gaps and one introducing 8 gaps. In the 2 gap cases, the width of the gaps is
different thereby resulting in a different reactivity worth for the two experiments. With the
explicit modeling of the boundaries, the first experiment is the hardest to deal with as it
introduces the most boundaries. Table 91 tabulates the axial boundaries used to construct Model
7 for Loading 206. As can be seen, both the expanded and un-expanded configurations use the
same axial meshing to avoid spatial mesh refinement contamination in the computed results.
Similarly, Table 92and Table 93 provide the axial meshing used to construct Model 7 for the two
experiments that introduce two gaps.

 The three measured worths for experiment 1, 2, and 3 are -0.689 pcm ± 5.83%, -0.707
pcm ± 5.96%, and -0.463 pcm ± 5.90%, respectively. As was the case with Loading 117, the
DIF3D code was not able to produce a transport result when using an energy group structure
greater than 33 groups for two of the experiments. For the 8 gap problem, DIF3D was unable to
produce a transport result for any of the preferred energy group structures and thus only diffusion
results are possible. Table 94 provides the results computed using DIF3D for all three
experiments using Model 7. As can be seen, for experiment 2, a transport solution was possible
with DIF3D using 116 groups. In that case, DIF3D produced an identical solution as that
obtained with the 33 group structure when using the same space-angle settings. This indicates
that the number of energy groups used to calculate the reactivity worth is not impactful. This is
a similar finding to the anisotropic scattering kernel and spatial approximations.

 Because the diffusion theory results for Model 7 are known to be unreliable, the large
errors obtained for Experiment 1 are of no surprise. Looking over the results for Loading 117

 108

and now Loading 206, it is rather clear that using diffusion theory over predicts the reactivity
worth which is indicative of the diffusion theory methodology over predicting leakage on a wide
variety of past reactor analysis cases. Focusing on just the transport results, a rather consistent
17% over prediction is occurring between experiment 1 and 2 where 7% was obtained for
Loading 117. Because the diffusion theory results are not consistent between experiments 1
through 3, no reasonable assumption can be made on how much error would result when using
transport on experiment 1.

 As was the case with Loading 117, it is not possible to compute the measurements taken
in Loading 206 with MCNP. Thus, as was done with Loading 117, a hypothetical loading termed
206mod48 was created in a similar fashion to that done in Figure 76 where 48 expanding drawers
were added to the stationary side of the assembly. In this loading, all 49 of the expandable
drawers have the same geometry as that used in Loading 206 and the expansion used in each
experiment is maintained consistently across all of the drawers. There is no experimental basis
for this loading and it is merely used to get a comparison result with MCNP. The MCNP
solutions obtained using explicit geometry models were -38±1 pcm, -40±1 pcm, and -27±1 pcm,
for experiment 1, 2, and 3, respectively. Table 95 provides the DIF3D calculated results for the
same Loading 206mod48 with the error calculated with respect to each MCNP reference
solution.

 As was the case with the Model 7 results for Loading 206, DIF3D is unable to calculate
the experiment 1 using transport theory with the preferred energy group structures and thus only
diffusion theory results are available. Contrary to this, DIF3D is able to apply transport to
experiment 2 using a 33 group and 116 group structure which have nearly identical results when
using the same space-angle approximations. Much like Loading 117mod48, the DIF3D and
MCNP results for this experiment are very consistent and indicate that the problem does not
likely lie with the DIF3D modeling efforts. While diffusion theory is clearly inadequate from all
of the preceding results, with respect to the measured results, there is an underlying cross section
issue or perhaps a mistake in the experiment itself or the interpretation of the experiment with
regard to creating the DIF3D model. Since these aspects cannot be determined given the

 109

available information, no further study or work is taken to improve the results shown in Table
89 or Table 94.

12. Analysis of Doppler Sample Reactivity Worth Measurements
 Whole core Doppler reactivity worth measurements were not feasible in ZPPR because
a) measuring the ZPPR temperature distribution accurately would have required a very large
number of thermocouples, b) it was not possible to perform the calculations required to account
for thermal expansion of the plates, drawers and matrix tubes resulting from heating and c) safety
considerations would have limited the allowed heating. Doppler sample reactivity worth
measurements were performed in ZPPR-15A, ZPPR-15B, and ZPPR-15D [23]. Unlike whole
core Doppler reactivity worth, the Doppler sample allowed for much more controlled
measurements of the Doppler worth as the temperature change of the sample could be accurately
measured. In the experiment, a Doppler sample was loaded into the Doppler mechanism which
consisted of a heating element surrounded by an outer capsule. The temperature of the sample
was measured with a thermocouple inserted into a small cylindrical cavity in the center of the
sample. The Doppler mechanism was placed in a tray that itself was placed in a matrix tube at
the designated core location. The tray was long enough to extend through both halves of the
matrix. The worth of the sample was measured by measuring the reactivity change as the
Doppler mechanism and sample were oscillated into and out of the center of the core. The worth
of the heating element, capsule and other components of the mechanism was measured by
repeating the measurement without the Doppler sample present in the mechanism. The
difference between the measured worth with and without the Doppler sample determines the
worth of just the Doppler sample itself. Measurements were obtained at various temperatures
between 300 K and ~1100 K.

 Figure 78 shows a cross-sectional view of the Doppler sample, equipment and tray.
Figure 79 shows the a cross-sectional view of just the Doppler sample. Four Doppler samples
were measured in ZPPR-15 although the specific set of samples measured in each phase was
different. Doppler sample N3 consisted of natural UO2, sample N11 consisted of depleted

 110

uranium metal, sample N24 consisted of depleted U-10Zr (depleted uranium metal alloyed with
10 wt.% Zr), and sample E33-A consisted of 33% enriched UO2. Each sample had a diameter
of 2.54 cm. Samples N3, N24 and E33-A were 30.5 cm long while sample N11 was 25.4 cm
long. Samples N3 and N11 were measured in loading 43 of ZPPR-15A. Samples N3, N11, and
N24 were measured in loading 139 of ZPPR-15B. All four samples were measured in loading
207 of ZPPR-15D. Further details on the samples can be found in reference [23].

 Figure 78. Cross-Sectional View of Doppler Experimental Equipment

 Figure 79. Cross-Sectional View of a Doppler Sample

 While BuildZPRmodel produces MC2 inputs for all regions in the DIF3D model, the
generation of the Doppler sample cross sections requires additional effort. The input to
BuildZPRmodel included a dummy Doppler sample drawer. Because BuildZPRmodel does not

 111

deal with different temperatures (only 300K), it cannot directly build the MC 2 input for the
Doppler sample drawer (1002). After some testing, the most accurate method to obtain multi-
group cross sections for the Doppler sample involved creating a 1D radial model in MC 2. The
inner cylinder would be the homogenized drawer 1002 mixture while the surrounding annulus
would be a fully homogenized composition from the drawers surrounding the 1002 drawer in
the real loading. The central cylinder has an ~3.12 cm radius and the remainder of the geometry
was arbitrarily set to 50 cm to ensure the 3.12 cm section was of little importance to the total
flux solution. The loading details of each ZPPR-15A, -15B, and -15D were inspected and a single
drawer type was typically present surrounding the Doppler sample location.

 For ZPPR-15A, drawer type 1002 was surrounded by drawer type 102. This drawer input
was taken from the full core BuildZPRmodel input and used to create a 2x2 loading that
BuildZPRmodel could process to construct homogenized MC 2 models (of drawer 102 and 1002).
The BuildZPRmodel inputs for drawer 1002 for the N03, N11, N24, and E33 Doppler samples
were constructed by preserving the cylindrical area with squares as shown in Figure 80 (matrix
tube, Doppler sample drawer, and Doppler sample are displayed. This aspect is immaterial to the
actual MC2 cross section model other than being the means to exactly obtain the fully
homogenized compositions put into the 1D radial MC 2 model.

 Figure 80. Cross-Sectional View of the BuildZPRmodel Drawer Input

 It should be noted that the reported worths by the experimenters were given in cents/kg
where kg refers to the mass of U-238 in the sample. The atom densities of the samples and the
total mass of U-238 in each sample was provided while the total measured mass of the sample
was not. This is a combination of very poor decisions as the measurement itself was done in
cents and the atom densities, combined with the reported dimensions of the samples, yield
inconsistencies with the reported mass of U-238 as detailed in Table 96. It should also be noted
that the mass density of the samples computed with the stated atom densities is not consistent
with the known mass densities of the described compositions. For example, for samples N03 and
E33, the UO2 density using the reported atom densities is 8.25 g/cm 3 and 7.45 g/cm3, respectively
where the correct density should be closer to 10 g/cm 3. The N11 and N24 samples have densities
reasonably similar to those expected, but the N24 sample has a reported U-238 mass which is
well below the mass calculable with the given atom density information. Arbitrarily dividing all
of the measured results by some calculated U-238 mass, or really any computed quantity, directly
causes problems with the modeling effort here. Other issues with the results are that the
experimentalists corrected the temperature results based upon a fit of the measured data to
account for phase changes along with corrections for axial expansion of the sample.

 112

 With all of the preceding problems outlined, for the analysis work here, the mass values
reported by the experimentalists are assumed to be those used to create the cents/kg reported
measured results. The fact that there is a disagreement in mass when using the atom densities
provided by the experimentalists and the sample dimensions is assumed to be a uncorrected
mistake by the experimentalists when reporting their results. In effect we assume the
experimentalists measured the total sample mass and calculated atom densities that were
consistent with that mass and the geometry they assumed for their model which they provided
dimensions on. The disagreement of the provided atom densities with those resulting from
typical densities of those materials is assumed to be a result of voids or other non-reported
aspects of the Doppler samples which decrease the effective material density. In the end, the
error in the mass can be considered translatable to an error in the measured worth. The worst
error is for N24 which is 10% while the rest are less than 2%.

 The total sample worth was measured at 300K and reported in each loading. The DIF3D
calculated worth and the measured worth of all samples in ZPPR-15A (loading 43), ZPPR-15B
(loading 139), and ZPPR-15D (loading 207) are provided in Table 97 for a range of space-angle-
energy approximations in DIF3D. The spatial approximation notation used previously of F6-Z4-
S6-L1 is replaced here with the DIF3D input of 60621. The errors on the measured worth were
modified from the original reported errors to include a 5% error associated with the uncertainty
in β that is used as part of the conversion of measured reactivity versus calculated reactivity and
the error from the sample reported mass detailed above. This latter aspect is not statistical and
thus the modified experimental errors reported in Table 97 should not be viewed as fully
statistical in nature. Because only sample N24 has a large error in the reported mass, the modified
error provided in in Table 97 effectively becomes the 5% error assumed in the β unit conversion.

 Starting with the N03 sample in Table 97, one finds that the sample worth is rather
consistent for the three loadings but the error profile is not. The DIF3D computed results for

 113

loading 139 are considerably worse than the other two loadings where the measurement error of
5%. What is interesting to note is that the sample has a non-trivial dependence upon the angular
approximation. For the P3P1 to P5P3 transition at 33 groups, the DIF3D calculated result is
observed to increase by 4-5%. The P3P1 results for 33, 116, and 230 groups are all very similar
for all of the samples indicating that the group structure is likely not a driving factor in the worth
calculation. The importance of the angular approximation is likely due to the fact that a bulk of
the Doppler sample drawer position is filled with void and thus there is non-trivial leakage for
the Doppler sample worth calculation.

well within the modified measurement error.

 Looking at the N3 Doppler sample worth, the comparison is rather good with less than
 10% error for the bulk of the DIF3D results. The highest error is 12.4% and using the highest
 energy group structure one gets errors below 9.6%. The 33 group results have errors in the 3-7%
 range. The spatial approximation change at 116 groups has almost no impact on the worth
 calculations. It is unusual to see the anisotropic scattering kernel have an impact on the
 calculated worth but this is clearly occuring for the N3, 116 group structure results. It is noted
 that the 33 group structure shows a much smaller dependence on the anisotropic scattering and
 the N11 result for 116 groups shows less of an impact. The actual errors in the calculated worth

 115

 are on the order of ~0.01 pcm which is very small for a typical eigenvalue problem with DIF3D
 and thus does not warrant attempting higher space-angle-energy approximations for the purposes
 of this report.

 Continuing with the N11 sample results, the errors with respect to the reported results are
 mostly similar to those seen with the N3 sample but still well within 10% on the bulk of the
 results. Combined, the N3 and N11 sample results can be considered accurate enough given the
 very small worth of the actual measurement, the non-trivial measurement and unit conversion
 errors.

 12.3 ZPPR-15D Doppler Sample Reactivity Worth

 The last set of Doppler sample measurements was done in loading 207 of ZPPR-15D.
 As stated earlier, all four Doppler samples (N3, N11, N24 and E33-A) were measured in this
 loading. While loading 43 and 139 both contain Pu dominated fuel forms, loading 207 is an
 enriched uranium fuel form (Pu is present in the loading for source multiplication needs). In
 some cases, especially the E-33A sample, the measured worth is so low that it likely is unusable
 for validation purposes as will be discussed shortly.

 The reference temperature was again 300 K and each Doppler sample measurement
 involved 5 temperature variations. Table 103, Table 104, Table 105, and Table 106 provide the
 N3, N11, N24, and E33-A Doppler sample worth results for DIF3D, respectively. The same
 space-angle-energy approximations used in the preceding results were used for these samples.
 The N03 sample has a maximum error of 9.9% and smallest error of 6.2% with the typical error
 being 7%. The N11 sample has a maximum error of 16% and minimum error of 12%. The N24
 sample has errors between 21% and 23% while the E33 sample has errors ranging between 11%
 and 24%. A quick survey of all of the results indicates that they have larger errors than those
 observed in loading 43 and 139.

 Starting with the N3 sample, the measured Doppler results are similar in magnitude to
 those taken during loading 43 and 139. For the N11 sample, the magnitude of the errors is
 consistent with that observed with loading 43 but notably higher than the loading 139 results.
 The N24 Doppler sample results in Table 105 are considerably larger than those in loading 139.
 The E33-A Doppler sample results in Table 106 are by far the worst results obtained in any of
 the loadings and samples. The average error is well above 15% with maximum errors of 24%.

 One common aspect of all of these measurements is that the errors in the calculated
 results are in the seventh significant digit of k-effective or ±0.0000001. Because the iterative
 error reported by DIF3D is typically less than 10-9 on the eigenvalue and 10-8 on the fission
 source, we should be able to rule this out as a reason for the accuracy issues. What cannot be
 ruled out as sources of error are the cross section generation methodology for the Doppler sample
 and how those cross sections are used by DIF3D.

 Because all of the other cross sections generated for the other drawers are identical
 between the base (300 K) and perturbed state (higher temperature), those can be ruled out as the
 dominate sources of error. A much more complicated cylindrical model was constructed for the
 Doppler sample in MC2-3 and it did improve the Doppler sample results but not drastically (max
 24% error dropped to 22%). What should be clear is that one can obviously manipulate the cross
 section generation scheme to manipulate the output results of DIF3D and it is difficult to argue
 which scheme is applicable in a real reactor model. In that regard, the current simple model was
 retained.

 With regards to how DIF3D uses the data, there are several things to consider. First, the
 ISOTXS file produced by MC2-3 is real precision and thus some amount of truncation error
 should occur although this is typically not a source of error. To check it, the cross section
 difference between the 300K and 1083K temperatures of the E33 sample were compared and the
 maximum difference was 10% in a few groups for U-235 fission and 39% in the U-238 (n,γ)
 reaction for a few groups. These large changes in the cross section values should dominate the
 actual worth and they would not be impacted by the real precision storage of the cross section
 data.

 Another aspect to consider is how DIF3D uses the cross section data. Because the worth
 is small (less than 0.4 pcm), the first order perturbation theory option of PERSENT was used to
 get the Doppler sample results and it was found to produce much worse errors (typically double
 the direct calculation) indicating that the changes in the flux solution cannot be ignored. Because
 those changes only impact the zones that were perturbed, this means the flux solution changes
 in the Doppler sample meshes cannot be ignored when it comes to calculating the worth. This is
 a very unusual outcome of a worth calculation that is this small. In that regard, another aspect
 that cannot be eliminated as a source of error is that DIF3D uses a compositional χ treatment.

 119

This treatment has a non-uniform impact on the fission source part of the operator and might be
a source of error for this type of small worth calculation. Normally this should not contribute
much to the overall error, but it cannot be ruled out because the first order result did so poorly.

 Other potential sources of error are the fact that the Doppler samples do not report the U-
236 or U-234 content while several of the uranium plates in ZPPR do have these details. One
would again not normally consider this to be a substantial issue, however, given the small worth
measured for this sample, such mistakes can impact the overall calculated results compared
against the experimental measurements. Because of the errors made by the experimenters in
reporting the equipment being used and the measurement details, this set of Doppler sample
measurements should not be considered the gold standard for software validation. Even with the
stated issues, using the assumptions made about the samples and equipment results in DIF3D
producing results that are resonably accurate with respect to the measured sample worths.

12.4 Modifications to the Doppler Sample Loadings

 For all of the Doppler sample measurements in ZPPR-15A, ZPPR-15B, and ZPPR-15D,
only a single sample drawer was present and thus the measured reactivity worth was small (<1
pcm). None of these sample measurements are directly solvable with MCNP (the perturbation
theory option of MCNP might be usable but was not tested). Consequently, this section focuses
on creating Doppler sample loadings that allow the MCNP code to produce a comparable result
and assessing the DIF3D methodology in that regard. A hypothetical loading is created such that
63 additional sample drawers were introduced into each loading (thus 64 total sample drawers
were modeled). Figure 81 shows an example modification of loading 43 of ZPPR-15A, termed
loading 43mod63 where the modification to ZPPR-15B (139mod63) and ZPPR-15D
(207mod63) are similar in concept and not shown for brevity. All of the DIF3D calculations in
this section are done with a 116 energy group structure and a P 3 angular flux and P3 scattering
kernel.

 Because the sample drawer does not contain fuel, or it is not similar to the core drawer
that was replaced, this leads to a severe reduction in criticality compared with the actual
experimental loading. All 64 sample drawers were setup to have the same geometry and
composition. As was done for the axial expansion case, there is no experimental basis for these
hypothetical loadings and MCNP is assumed to produce the reference solution given it has the
best representation of the underlying geometry. To eliminate problems with evaluated cross
section libraries in MCNP and to get a large enough Doppler reactivity worth, a single
temperature change from 300 K to 1200 K was calculated for all samples in the hypothetical
loadings.

 To begin, Table 97 indicated that the total sample worth had some nominal amount of
error although the details of the experiment make the DIF3D comparison rather suspect. To
verify this, the total sample worth for the modified loading cases was solved using MCNP and
DIF3D and the results are tabulated in Table 107.

The statistical error on all of the MCNP calculations was ±1 pcm and thus the statistical error on
the worth is also small (< 2 pcm). As can be seen, the DIF3D and MCNP results produce a large
sample worth for all samples in all loadings. With the exception of loading 207mod63, the
sample worth comparison between DIF3D and MCNP is exceptionally good. In the higher error
cases of the N3, N11, and N24 samples, this problem is believed to be a result of differing
spectrum between the modified loadings 43 and 139 and loading 207. It is noted that the enriched
sample in this case is very accurate with DIF3D whereas the depleted uranium samples all have
high errors. Based upon the other results, this is most likely a shortcoming in the Doppler sample
cross section generation methodology constructed for these loadings.

 As was done with the total sample worth, the Doppler sample worth was calculated using
DIF3D and MCNP and the results are tabulated in Table 108 (300K to 1200K temperature
change). The MCNP one-sigma statistical error for each calculation is ±1 pcm and thus the three-
sigma statistical error on the worth is small (~4.2 pcm). However, because the Doppler sample
worth is small compared with Table 107, the statistical error from MCNP on the Doppler sample
worth is non-trivial for all of the manufactured cases. A quick look at the DIF3D results indicates
that some of the samples have errors consistent with the Doppler sample results shown earlier
but in this case all of the DIF3D results fall within the 4.2 pcm error bars on the MCNP worth.

the lack of a trend in the actual Doppler sample measurements. All of the sample worth results
are acceptable given the statistical error on the MCNP result and the difficulty involved in
computing the sample worth. This work increases the confidence that the preceding calculation
of the Doppler samples is as good as it can be assuming that the modeled experiment is consistent
with the real experiment.
13.Analysis of TLD Loadings
 There are four ZPPR-15 experiments that covered TLD measurements: ZPPR-15B
Loading 115, 122 and 133 and ZPPR-15D Loading 202. Problems with the gamma related
solutions for EBR-II, ZPPR, and other reactors led to the identification of mistakes in the
processing of the ENDF cross section data that was available in MC 2. This section discusses the
analysis of TLD measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 115 and 133 and in ZPPR-15D Loading
202 using the recently updated MC2 gamma library.

13.1 Background on TLD Measurements

 A thermoluminescent dosimeter (TLD) consists of a thermoluminescent material in a
capsule. The thermoluminescent material contains a number of electron or hole traps which are
stable at room temperature. Some of these traps are filled when ionizing radiation creates
electrons or holes in the thermoluminescent material. When the irradiated thermoluminescent
material is subsequently heated, the electrons or holes are released from the trapping centers by
thermal ionization. Ionized carriers move through the thermoluminescent material until electron-
hole annihilation occurs. This annihilation is accompanied by emission of a photon.

 122

Measurement of the number and intensity of these photons provides an estimate of the radiation
dose received by the thermoluminescent material during irradiation.

 There are several thermoluminescent materials and various possible capsule materials.
Stainless-steel encapsulated 7LiF thermoluminescent dosimeters were used to measure gamma
doses as part of the ZPPR-15 experiments [26]. The Li in the LiF was enriched to approximately
99.993% Li-7 because neutron reactions with Li-6 obscure the gamma contribution to the
measured dose. For convenience, TLD will refer either to the LiF thermoluminescent material
inside the capsule or to the LiF material plus the stainless steel capsule in this report.

 Two types of dosimeters were used in the ZPPR-15 experiments. The first type was a
stainless steel cylindrical capsule with a diameter of 12.7 mm and height of 6.35 mm which could
contain up to five LiF disks. Two LiF disks were placed in each capsule for the ZPPR
experiments. The recorded measurement was the average of these two LiF disks. The dimensions
of the LiF disks were not listed in available reports, but for realistic capsule wall thicknesses, it
is likely that each LiF disk was a circular disk or chip with a diameter of ~10 mm and a thickness
of ~0.5 mm. These TLDs were placed in cavities in special tunnel sodium plates, i.e., sodium
plates with a 12.7 mm cavity, which replaced normal sodium plates at selected locations in the
assembly. These TLDs were used in the core and blanket.

 The second type of dosimeter was a stainless-steel clad cylindrical capsule with an outer
diameter of 3.175 mm and height of 12.7 mm which contained one LiF rod. The rod had a 1 mm
square cross section and height of 6 mm. The thickness of the capsule wall was ~0.035 in. or
~0.889 mm, and the steel was Type 304 stainless steel. The second type of dosimeter was
sandwiched between normal material plates for the gamma measurements.

 Type 304 stainless steel was widely used at ZPPR-15, so it is likely that the capsule for
the first type of dosimeter also consisted of Type 304 stainless steel. The capsule wall thickness
was a very important parameter for TLD measurements, so it is probable that the capsule wall
thickness for the first type of dosimeter also was ~0.889 mm.

13.2 Analysis of TLD Measurements in Loadings 115 and 133 of ZPPR-15B and in Loading
    202 of ZPPR-15D

 The TLD irradiations were performed such that the integrated reactor power was about
300 watt-hours, and integrated counts from the ex-core BF3 detectors provided power
normalization. Dosimeters were removed from the assembly 20 hours after reactor shutdown.

 The TLDs for the gamma dose distribution measurement were located in half 1. Figure
82 to Figure 84 show the matrix maps for half 1 of ZPPR-15B loading 115 and 133 and ZPPR-
15D loading 202; the directions along which TLD measurements were performed are indicated
with a dashed line. In Figure 83, the layout of loading 133 is cut at column 73 (reflector), however
the TLD measurements in the X direction extended up to column 76 in the reflector. The detailed
location of the TLDs in each ZPPR loading and the DIF3D model are provided in Appendix D4.

 123

Figure 82. Directions of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 115

 124

 Figure 83. Direction of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 133

 125

 Figure 84. Direction of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15D Loading 202

 For the calculations of the gamma dose rates, MC 2 was used to produce both neutron and
gamma cross section files. As implied from the preceding description, the TLD measurements
are provided as absolute measurements. Doing a direct comparison with DIF3D would require
applying the same power level but this introduces many sources of error from just the power
normalization of DIF3D/MC2 itself into the comparison. Thus, for the comparison work here, a
normalization value is computed by setting the calculated gamma dose rate at the core center
equal to the corresponding experimental value in the L115 and L202 case. For L133, the

 126

normalization is performed by setting the calculated gamma dose rate to the corresponding
measured value at the location where the maximum measurement in single fuel column (SC)
assemblies occurs. This converts the absolute measured TLD data into a relative comparison
which is similar to what was done with the spectral indices measurements provided earlier in
this report. Neutron cross sections are processed in 230 groups while the gamma cross sections
are obtained in 21 and 94 groups. Because the data is converted to a relative comparison, there
should be no real differences in the two libraries resulting from the data library correction but
the refined energy group structure should indicate any lack of fidelity in the coarser one.

 While the neutron cross sections of fissile zones are generated in slab geometry to
account for the plate heterogeneity of the drawer, the gamma cross section files can be only
obtained with homogeneous (0D) cell calculations. The GAMSOR code [11] is used to obtain
both the neutron and gamma flux distribution in the ZPPR-15 reactor. GAMSOR consists of
three steps: 1) calculate the neutron flux distribution, 2) obtain the gamma source distribution
from the neutron reactions, 3) obtain the gamma flux distribution. The DIF3D utility program
EvaluateFlux is used to evaluate the neutron flux and gamma flux at the exact centroid of the
each TLD. These flux values are then merged with the LiF heating (eV-barn) cross section data
generated by MC2-3.

 Table 109 shows the comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the X-
axis, and Table 110 shows the comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the
Y-axis of ZPPR-15B loading 115. The comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates
along the X- and Y-axis of ZPPR-15B loading 115 is also plotted in Figure 85 and Figure 86,
respectively. Besides the centroid location of each TLD, the tables provide additional details on
the drawer master associated with the measurements, such as the cell type and the reactor region
it is located. Concerning the cell type, the tables differentiate the symmetric (SCS) from the
asymmetric (SCA) single fuel column assemblies in the inner core, where the SCS assemblies
have two symmetric zirconium plates and SCA assemblies have a single asymmetric zirconium
plate (see e.g., the schematic view of drawer 158 vs drawer 161 in Appendix C). In the outer
core, the tables differentiate the double fuel column (DC) from the single fuel column (SC)
assemblies (see e.g., the schematic view of drawer 204 vs drawer 121 in Appendix C). The
experimental uncertainties listed in Table 109 and Table 110 are the statistical uncertainties of
the measurements and not the total uncertainties for the TLD measurements. Calculated values
were obtained with both gamma libraries in 21 and 94 energy groups but the results were
practically identical and only the 21 group results are presented here.

 In Figure 85 and Figure 86, the gamma rate values are indexed to the left axis while the
error (“σ relative (Exp.)” is the measurement error and “Error (GAMSOR-Exp.)” is the
discrepancy between the calculated and measured value) is indexed to the right axis. The figures
also indicate the reactor zones where the measurements are performed: inner core (IC), outer
core (OC), blanket (BK), reflector (RF). The comparison shows that the calculated gamma dose
rates agree quite well with the experimental values except for some discrepancies observed in
the outer core region in correspondence of double fuel column (DC) assemblies.
